It was the only thing on the floor that was. It crept. It spread down from the second knot onto the third, and round the sides of the column to the back, where the grey had been only grey. It went as breath goes across a cold window, all the way to the edges. The whole string began to shine, faintly, from the top to the legs.

There was no back to it now. There was no place behind the edge of the skin, because the skin had no edge.

And Kiva felt it.

It came up through the cinder and the soles of her boots, forty paces off. A little tap. A space. A little tap.

It was the thing that had been in the bag. It was in the floor.

She looked at the gratings. She was too far away to see through them. But the nearest person to the string was a Team Twelve ward-holder, and he was looking straight down between his own feet with his mouth open.

"There's something gone down," he said. "Through the bars. It's let something down. It's got hold of the—"

"*Vane!*"

It was the old keeper, and he had knocked over his stool.

"I've a load on the race that isn't on my book! All twelve! They've gone into step, man, look at them, they're ticking like a row of clocks!"

Kiva looked at the glass case.

She could not read a dial at that distance. She did not need to. Twelve needles caught the light together, and dropped it together, and caught it again. Above them, the lamps of the great board had begun to pulse. *FIFTH COMPANY,* it said, brighter and dimmer, brighter and dimmer, in time with a thing standing on a drain.

Nobody on the terraces was laughing now.

"Event's struck," said Hollis Vane.

He said it flatly, for the slate, and chalked as he spoke.

"Ten twenty-two. This is a response and it's mine. Students dark and off the race. Back to the east wall, all of you, and stand where I can count you. Graywater. You're in."

---

They came through the west gate at a run.

It was the first time Kiva had seen anyone in slate blue hurry. Four ward-holders spread across the floor in a line with their hands coming up as they went, and Doran Mace was behind them, and the silver that rose in front of the string was nothing like the students' wall. It had no seams. It was as bright as a shop window at night.

"Give it a gate," said Mace. "Five paces. Hold."

They held.

It was four Torch wards at five paces. Kiva had seen that pull a Flask the length of a meadow as a magnet pulls a nail.

The shining column swayed over the gratings. It did not turn.

"It's not reading," said one of the four. She had a broken nose. "Mace, there's nothing coming off it. It's like holding a lamp up to a wall."

The tall woman with the white streak had not run.

She walked in through the gate after the others with her hands out of her pockets. She went round the thing, once, at a long pace's distance, all the way round, looking up. Her right fist was dark. She stopped where she had begun.

"There's no door in it," said Rhea Sorn. Her voice was low and rough, and it carried. "It's skinned all round. I could light on it till supper." She looked down at the bars under its legs. "It's on your current, Hollis. It's got a tune under it. It isn't listening to us because it's found something better."

"Can you cut what's gone through the grating?"

"Not from the top. The iron's in my way." She frowned. "A ward might. Slid in flat, under the belly, across the bars. If the cords are only ash, a wall will shear them off the copper."

"Stroud," said Mace.

The woman with the broken nose stepped out of the line.

She did it well. Even from the table Kiva could see that. She went down on one knee at the edge of the race, a pace from a leg as thick as a bollard, and brought her two hands down low, palms to the cinder. Her ward came out of them thin and flat, like a baker's peel. She slid it in under the hanging knot, along the top of the gratings, toward the grey ropes.

It touched them.

Kiva had seen open ash take a pattern. She had seen it drink a lit blade in a brick cold room, and a pump tune in a hut. She had never seen it done backward.

The silver did not shear anything. It went *in.* The cords took the edge of that ward as a wick takes oil, and the brightness ran along it, the wrong way, out of the wall and up the woman's arms.

Greta Stroud screamed once. It was short. Then she was lying on her side on the cinder, curled round her own two hands, and there was smoke coming off them.

"*Down!*" said Mace. "Close the line. Nobody touches that with a pattern. Nobody!"

"Renn." Vane had not moved from where he stood. "You're a Carry. Get her out."

Kiva saw Kellan come off the east wall.

He did not light anything. He did not Stride. He ran, on his own feet, with his hands open, straight in under the flank of a seven-knot Flask to where a woman lay a pace from its leg. He went down on one knee and got her across his shoulders as a sack is got, and stood, and walked.

"Carry," he said. She heard it. He had declared it, to nobody, because that was what one did.

He took her north and west, toward the Graywater gate, and did not look round.

---

The string moved.

It was not much. One thick leg lifted, and came down a pace to the south. Another followed it. It was the slow rolling tread that Kiva had watched four still-doors use in a brick lane, the tread of something that has nowhere to hurry to. It walked straddling the race, three legs to a side, and it went south.

"It's going down the line," said the keeper. He had got to his feet, and his voice had cracked. "It's following the copper. Vane! It's going for my tunnel!"

"What's in your tunnel?"

"The trunk, man! The bank! Four-and-twenty cells with that tick in every one of them, and the pumps hung off them!" Dunmore's keys were jangling. "If it gets a cord past my link, I can't cut it off from this side. I'll have to quench the lot. The whole bank. And the Ferren's a yard over the weir this week."

Kiva knew what that sentence meant. She had read it out, a line at a time, at a kitchen table.

*Was it closed. Who was hurt. What still works in the morning.*

"Then open your link," said Vane.

"I'm going! Four minutes!" The old man was already at his gate, with his head thrust forward, at something that was meant to be a run.

"Rhea. Can you slow it?"

"With what? I've nothing it wants."

"Then it's the lid first." Vane's voice went up at last, a hard flat bark that struck the terraces and came back. "Every still-door on this floor to the capstan. Now. Brask. Cade. You, and you. If that branch goes dark with the Kiln standing open, I'd like there to be iron in the doorway. *Run.*"

They ran, as still-doors run. It was like watching furniture leave a room. Kiva saw Perrin go, heavy and fast, with a hand clamped to his ribs, and the shaved head of Lowen Brask beside him. Two more came off the east wall after them.

And she was looking at the gratings.

She had not stopped. The line of iron ran straight down the floor from under those six legs to the lip of the sump, fourteen paces west of the table where she stood, and through the nearest bars she could see into the channel.

There was grey in it.

It was moving. It came along the copper in the bottom of the race like water finding its way down a gutter, two ropes of it, one to each bar. It was well ahead of the legs. It had reached out before the body had taken its first step.

She watched it pass under a grating. She counted.

*One. Two. Three. Four.*

It passed under the next.

A grating was a pace. A pace was four counts. She looked up the line to where the grey began, and down it to the sump, and counted iron.

Fifteen.

*Sixty counts.*

She had done this sum before. She had done it on a mezzanine with her palm on a drum, and a needle going up a tenth at a time, and three people who would not be out of a pump room when it got to the top. She had been right then. It had not helped.

Sixty counts to the link. Four minutes to the keeper.

And the link was a sprung iron lever on a shelf across three paces of open sump. It wanted four hundredweight to bring it level. *One good still-door with his boot on the shelf.* There was no way onto that shelf but a door in a wall that an old man had not reached yet.

Every still-door in the Pit was running north.

It would not have mattered if they had been running south. They could not have crossed. A still-door could no more jump that trench than a wardrobe could. An Ember could have been over it in a breath, any Ember, Tolliver or Dray or that First Division girl. An Ember could have hung on the lever with all sixty kilos of her and not shifted it the width of a thumb.

The thing wanted somebody who could cross like the one and stand like the other.

Kiva stood at a deal table with a sealed bag and a whole dram in her that she had not touched.

*No,* she thought. It was quite clear. *It's not on my paper. I've a knee I'm not to land on till the second of next month. Fourteen tries. I'd sign none of them, she said.*

*Fifty-two.*

The grey slid under another grating.

She was not going to do it. She saw that with relief. She was going to do the thing she was certified for, which was to tell somebody who was in charge.

"*Foreman!*"

Her voice cracked on it. The Pit took it and threw it up the terraces. Vane's head came round.

"It's ahead of itself! The cords! They're in the race, fifteen gratings from the sump, a grating every four counts!" She pointed. Her hand was not steady. "That's sixty. Fifty now. The keeper's four minutes!"

He looked. He looked down the line of iron as she had, and she watched him do the same sum, and get the same answer, and not like it.

His pale eyes swept the south end of the floor. They went over the sump, and the sheer yellow wall, and the iron shelf. They went over a trestle table, and a clerk in an apron with his hands up to his mouth.

They stopped.

"Who's south of it?" said Hollis Vane.

She could have said *the clerk.*

"Rowan," said Kiva. "Seven. Catcher."

---

## CHAPTER — books/book-2680-opening/manuscript/chapter-23.md

# Chapter 23 — Kiva: Ember, to Still

"Rowan," said Hollis Vane. He did not waste a breath on being surprised. "Can you put a still-door on that shelf?"

*Forty-eight,* said the part of her that was counting iron.

"There's one way over, and it's a Stride." She had to shout it down forty paces of cinder, and the whole Pit heard. She found that she did not care. "I've both. One after the other. It isn't on my paper. I've tried it fourteen times and missed fourteen. The last four didn't hurt. One held for a count."

*Forty-four.*

It was the truest thing she had ever said aloud, and it was a list of failures, and she had said it to eleven hundred people.

"Dane!"

Orla was not in the first row. She had come down. Kiva saw her at the floor rail on the east side, both hands on the iron, with the stick hooked over her wrist. She must have started down the steps when the board began to pulse.

"I've not seen her land one," said Orla. Her voice was hard and clear, the voice of the Home Line twenty years ago. "I'll not put my name to it. I won't certify a thing I haven't watched."

"I didn't ask for a certificate. I asked what you've seen."

*Forty.*

"Fourteen misses. Every one called inside the breath, by her, before I'd opened my mouth." Orla did not look at Kiva. She looked at the officer. "If her line lights over that sump, she'll say *off* and go in it on her back before she'll stand a jam on your bus. That, I've seen. That, I'll sign."

"That isn't a certificate."

"No. It's a reason. Write it down." The knuckles on the rail were white. "And if it goes wrong, you write it as mine. I'll not have it written as hers."

*Thirty-six.*

Vane's chalk was already moving. He spoke as he wrote, flat and quick.

"Field direction. Ten twenty-three. Rowan, K. One attempt at the south link, door to door, declared. Reason: nobody else on this ground can stand on it, and it's thirty counts off." His eyes came up. "You'll call your own off. I'm told you're good at it. If your first door isn't shut before your foot's down, you go in the ditch. Dray!"

There was a movement at the reserves' rail.

"Here."

"You're a Stride. Over after her. There's a pin on a chain. When that lever's level, you drop it through the lower eye."

Selka Dray stood with her yellow card in her hand. For one count she did not move, and Kiva saw her face. It was the face of somebody who has been handed a job by the wrong person, for the wrong reason, on the one morning she had come to be proved right.

"Pin," said Dray. "Lower eye."

She put a hand on the rail and went over it.

"Go," said Vane.

---

It was thirteen paces to the lip of the sump.

Kiva took them at a hobble that was nearly a run. The knee howled. She let it. She was not going to need it for anything but leaving the ground.

*Thirty. Twenty-eight.*

She did not go to the end of the race. She went to a place on the brick lip a pace east of it, and stopped, and looked across.

Three paces of air. At the bottom of it, a man and a half down, two bars of bare copper ran down their ramp into the dark, and the grey had not reached them yet. Beyond, the wall went up sheer and yellow. The shelf was an iron grating a pace deep. The lever hung along the brick with its crossbar at the height of her eyes, and above it stood the two copper jaws with the blade bedded in them, wide as a pair of open hands.

There was room on that shelf for her two boots and nothing else.

*It isn't a blow,* she thought. *It's only weight, arriving.*

She would not be putting it into the lever. She had learned that on her face in a brick trough. The speed was going into the ground, through her, as it had always been going to. The lever came after, and that was only standing.

*Twenty-four.*

"Rowan, changing on three!" It went up the terraces like a thrown stone. "Ember, to still!"

Forty paces north, Hollis Vane's right hand went up over his head, with the chalk in it. He had no flag. He had read her file on a tram.

She did not wait for it. That was the rule. She waited for herself.

"*One.*"

The metal came off the cuff. It ran down her sides, cool and quick, and closed on her calves, and she bent the good knee and the bad knee together and left the ground.

The lip fell away. The sump opened under her, with its copper.

"*Two.*"

She shut it at the top.

The struts let go. She felt the metal run for home, up her legs and her ribs, and arrive. She had nothing. She was a girl in the air over a brick ditch with no doors at all, and the whole speed of a Stride in her like a stone in a sling, and a yellow wall coming.

It lasted as long as a word. She had fallen through that word fourteen times.

She did not listen for the iron. She would have been late.

She set the pan.

She set it under herself, before, as she had set it four hundred times under ash that had not yet fallen, in the dark, with two bad fingers. She opened the still door downward into a shelf that her boot had not reached, and held it there, wide and waiting, for the weight that was coming.

"*Three.*"

Her right foot came down into it.

The shelf rang like a struck rail. That was all. There was no jar. There was no landing. The bound went in at her heel and through her, the whole of it, and out by a road that had been ready, down through four bolts and into two hundred years of Brickfields wall.

Her left knee did not know that anything had happened.

She was standing on an iron grating with her shoulder a hand's width from the brick. She had not swayed. Nothing on earth could have moved her.

*Caught,* said her hands.

She did not put the lid on.

"Four," said Kiva.

She took the crossbar. She put her right fist round it and hooked her left arm over it at the elbow, because she knew to the hair what those two fingers would tell her. Then she let herself go down.

She did not pull. A still-door did not pull. She only became heavy, heavier than the shelf, as heavy as the wall, and the lever was in the way of it.

It came.

The spring groaned like a ship. The iron bar swung down along the brick through a slow quarter of a circle. Above her head the copper blade lifted out of its jaws with a crack and a spit of blue.

"Five."

Every lamp in the Pit went out.

She did not see them go. She felt it. The hum that had lain under that floor all morning stopped, as a held note stops. The great board was a black slab. And far off at the north end, something that had been pressing on the back of her neck through a lattice was suddenly pressing through nothing.

The lever was down. It was not level. She could see the lower eye in its bracket, and the hole in the bar, and a thumb's width of daylight between them. She had no more to give it. She was a first-year with a Hold of six and two, and one good arm.

"Six."

She looked down.

The grey had come out of the race. It lay on the copper on the ramp, three paces below her and a pace north, two ropes of it as thick as her arm. It had been reaching for the dark mouth in the wall.

It stopped.

It lay on bars that had nothing in them, and it stopped as a hand stops on a door that it expected to open. Then, all along the ramp, it let go. It slumped. It ran down off the copper into the bottom of the sump in a soft dry spill, and was only ash.

"Seven."

Twenty paces up the floor, the shine went off the string.

It went in a wave from the legs upward, as frost goes off a pane when the sun reaches it. The bloom drew in. It gathered to one face of the topmost knot, and sat there.

The knot swung. It was reading again.

It read for a worked thing. It found the nearest one there was. That was a stance, wide open, on a shelf across a ditch.

It turned to her.

It had no eyes. She had told a warden that once. But the whole blunt frosted top of a seven-knot Flask came round across the bright air until it was square on her, and stayed. One leg lifted. It came down a pace nearer the sump.

Her hands began to go.

It came up through the fear, as it always had. *Stride. Three bounds and you're off this shelf.* The Ember stirred against the cuff. Behind it, the hand door rose cold and wild, a ward, a tea tray's worth, anything to put between her and that.

If either of them opened, she would be standing over a live trunk on a jam.

"Eight," said Kiva. Her voice was a croak. "*Nine.*"

She gave them the numbers. She gave them to the Ember and to the hand door, one each, like crusts to dogs. She kept her eyes on the hole in the bar. The left arm had started to shake at the elbow.

Something landed beside her.

It was as light as a cat on a wall. The shelf hardly spoke. Dull yellow struts flexed and held, on the last half pace of grating that there was, and a hand shot past her shoulder for the pin on its chain.

"It's high," said Selka Dray. She was not out of breath. "It won't go. It's a thumb off. Get it down."

"I can't."

"Then move your elbow."

Two hands came down on the crossbar, outside her own. Dray picked her feet up off the shelf. She hung there with all sixty kilos of herself, like a child on a gate.

The thumb's width closed.

"Hold that. Hold it—"

One of the hands let go. There was a scrape of steel.

*Clack.*

"It's in." Dray's boots were on the iron again. "It's pinned. Let go."

"Yours?"

"It's the *pin's,* you—yes. Mine. Let go of it, Rowan!"

"Ten," said Kiva. "Eleven."

Up the floor, a wall of silver had come up like a sunrise. Three wards, bright and seamless, walked in to ten paces. The frosted face wavered. It hung between a girl on a shelf and three women in blue.

She gave the wall its weight back.

*Thank you.*

She said it to two hundred years of yellow brick. She shut the still door behind it, cleanly, with nothing left in the doorway, and stood on an iron shelf with no doors open at all.

The lever stayed where it was.

The Flask turned its face to the Graywater line, and walked north.

---

For a moment she only stood.

There was a very great deal the matter with her. She took it in order, as she would have read down a board. Her left arm hung from the shoulder like a wet towel, and the first two fingers were bone white. Her legs were shaking. There was something running over her lip.

And the dram was gone.

It was not all gone. But she could feel the place where it had been whole, an hour ago, at a gate. A third of it was simply missing. It had not gone into the bound. A bound cost nothing. It had not gone into the stance. She had stood four minutes on a stance for a tenth.

It had gone at the top. It had gone in the gap, in the time it took to say *two,* when she had shut one door and set the other and been nothing in between. Something had carried a Stride across that gap. Whatever it was had drunk a third of a dram to do it.

The lines on her chest were not burning. She put her right hand up to be sure. They had not fought. They ached, all three, the whole length to her arm, with the deep, stupid ache of a muscle that has been used for the first time in its life.

Her fingers were on her breastbone. They were on the lines.

There was something else there.

It was small. It was exactly round. It lay between the bottom line and the next, where it had lain for four years, and it was warm.

It was not the warmth of her own skin. She knew what her skin felt like through a shirt. This was warm as a hand is, when somebody lays it flat on your chest to feel whether you are breathing.

Kiva closed her fist round the disk through the cloth.

She did not ask it anything. She noticed that. She had nothing to ask. She only stood on a shelf with blood on her lip and held it, as you would hold a cup that somebody had put into your hands.

And the shelf went away.

---

She was high up. Very high.

They were coming. From every road. She could not see their faces, only their light, and no two lights were alike.

This time there was sound.

Each of them had a note. She had not known that before, or had not kept it. They were not in tune. They came on toward one another over a dark country, each humming its one note, old and bright and unlike.

She tried to count them.

She got as far as four and lost the number. She began again. It would not stay. She, who had counted through a husk walking at her, could not hold a figure in her head for the length of a breath.

The lights touched.

The notes did not make a chord. They made one note.

She looked up. There was no sky.

And the one note went on, with nothing over it, and what was above came down into it, full and enormous and paying attention.

Below her, very small and very exact, something answered.

*Tick.*

---

"—seventeen. Eighteen. Rowan. *Rowan!*"

Somebody had her by the belt.

She was against the wall. The brick was rough on her cheek. A fist was knotted in the back of her belt and another in her collar, and both were hauling her inward, hard, away from the edge of an iron grating that she was a good deal nearer to than she had been.

"You stopped counting," said Selka Dray, in her ear. Her voice was furious. "So I did it. Nineteen. Don't you dare go in that ditch. I've only just pinned this."

Kiva got her feet under her. The shelf was there. The Pit was there, dark and roaring.

"How long?"

"I don't know. Three counts. Four. You went white and started to lean." The grip on her collar did not ease. "Sit down. Against the wall. *Down.*"

She sat. There was nowhere else. She sat on the grating with her back to the brick and her bad leg sticking out over the sump, and found that her right fist was still closed at her chest.

It was still warm.

Dray was looking at the fist. Then she was looking at Kiva's face.

"You're bleeding," she said. "Your nose."

"I know."

"What have you got?"

"Nothing. It's—" She could not think of a lie. She had never been any good at them. "It's a thing I wear. It's warm. It isn't, generally."

Dray plainly did not think that was an answer. She let go of the collar, slowly, and stood with her back to the wall, a pace away, with her yellow struts gone home and her arms folded tight.

"The lamps jumped," she said.

"What?"

"Just now. When you went white. All of them, round the top. Once." Dray jerked her chin at the rim, where forty dead lamps stood on their posts against the morning. "There's no current in them. I pinned it myself. They jumped anyway."

Kiva looked up at the lamps. They were only lamps.

She had no answer to that either. She put it with the plate in the meadow and the tick in the bag, and held on to the disk.

---

Perrin Cade had asked, once, to be told before he was rescued.

He thought about that on the capstan. It was a poor time to think of anything, but his mind had always wandered when his body was busy, and his body just then was a boy leaning on an oak bar with two cracked ribs.

"Two to a bar," said Lowen Brask. "Cade, with me. Walk it. Nobody plants till I say."

There were eight of them. Four were the Hall's duty crew, third-years with necks, and two were strangers from the east wall, and one was Perrin, and one was the man who had taught him to fall. They walked the capstan round against its pawl, and the chains paid out over the wheels, and the iron lid came down in its frame a link at a time. *Clack. Clack.*

He knew this capstan like his mother's mangle. It took two minutes with four. With eight it ought to take one.

It was a third of the way down when he heard her.

It came the whole length of the Pit, thin and cracked and unmistakable. *Rowan, changing on three!*

He could not turn round. The bar was across his chest.

*She's telling everyone,* thought Perrin. *All eleven hundred. She might have just told me.*

"One," said the far voice. "Two."

He did not hear *three.* He heard the lamps go.

They went all together. One moment there was a ring of pale lights round the rim and a note in his back teeth that he had stopped noticing on Monday. The next there was neither. The note stopped as if it had been cut with scissors.

It was a great deal worse without it.

"Lattice is off!" That was the warden, somewhere in her shed. "It's off, it's off, I've nothing!"

The gap under the lid was as high as a man's waist. There was blue in it.

Then there was grey.

It came out under the iron low and blunt, a leg as thick as a quay bollard, feeling for the cinder. Another came beside it. Behind them, the deep tired blue bulged outward into the morning.

"That's a second one," said one of the duty crew. His voice had gone up like a girl's. "Brask, that's a second—"

"I see it. Walk."

They walked. The lid came down two more links, and met the top of a grey leg, and stopped.

The bar kicked back into Perrin's chest. Something in his side said a thing that he did not let out of his mouth.

"Plant," said Lowen Brask. "All of you. Now."

Eight still doors opened at once.

Perrin felt the platform take them. He had never been part of that before. It was like standing in a wall. The bar stopped kicking. The lid sat on the grey leg with the weight of two hundred years of cast iron and eight people who could not be moved, and the leg shoved upward, and nothing went either way.

"It's pushing," said Brask. He sounded interested. He always did. "Good. It's got its whole self on that. Listen to me, the lot of you. This is a caller's rule, and it isn't ours, and I learned what it's worth on Saturday. On three we give it the bar. One step back, all together. Then we take it again and we don't stop. Cade, what did I tell you every morning for a month?"

"Arrive before you're there," said Perrin, through his teeth.

"That's the one. One. Two. Three. *Give.*"

They gave.

The capstan spun back a quarter turn. The lid jumped up a hand's width in its frame. Beneath it, the leg that had been straining against a ton of iron found suddenly that there was no iron, and shot upward and out, and lost its footing on the cinder.

"*Take!*"

Eight still-doors drove the bars.

Perrin did not plant for it. Brask had not asked for a stance. He had asked for a walk with a wall's weight in it, and Perrin gave him that, and his ribs gave him the rest.

The lid came down. It came down the hand's width and a yard more, in one long rattle of chain, onto a thing that was off balance and half out of a doorway. It did not stop this time.

It seated in its stone sill with a boom that he felt in the soles of his feet.

On the cinder outside, a piece of grey as long as his arm lay by itself. It twitched once. Then it let go and poured, and was a heap of warm ash on the open floor with nobody to catch it.

"Sixty," said somebody, stupidly. "Somebody ought to count."

"That's sweepings," said Brask. "Let it lie. Don't any of you go lit near it." He had not taken his hands off the bar. "Stay planted. All of you. I'd like four minutes of standing on this before I believe it."

Perrin stood on it.

He had said *ow.* He was fairly sure that he had said it loudly. There was a hot wire in his side where two ribs had nearly finished knitting, and he suspected that they would now have to begin again.

A hand came down on his shoulder, broad and careful.

"I'm telling you first," said Lowen Brask. "I'm going to ask you to stand here four minutes more, on those ribs, because you're the only one on this bar who didn't have to be told twice. After that I'll carry you to the healer myself."

"I'd like it said that I mind."

"It's said."

Far off at the dark south end, a girl's voice was counting. It was not Kiva's. It sounded cross.

Perrin listened to it, and stood.

---

The lamps went out, and the thing in front of Rhea Sorn remembered that it was a husk.

She saw it happen. The shine drew in off the whole column and sat on one face of the top knot, a thick dull bloom. It was thicker than it had any right to be. It had not broken when the boy cut the first rung. It had slid, and she had seen it slide, and thought, *that's new,* and then the floor had gone dark and there had been no leisure for the thought.

It swung south. It had found something on the wall.

"Mace."

"I see it. Line, walk in. Ten paces. *Brighter.*"

He had three. Stroud was on a stretcher at the gate with her hands in wet cloth. Three Torch wards made a wall that was bright enough and not wide enough, and Rhea heard Mace say a name that was not on his roster.

"Pryce. Behind Imrie's shoulder. Hands below your belt. You're not in the line. If she says *take,* you take, for ten, and you give it back."

A girl in a plain dark coat came across the cinder at a walk and stood where she had been put.

The face came round to the wall. Rhea let out a breath.

She did not look south again. She had seen what there was to see, and she had a Flask. But it sat at the back of her eyes while she worked: a girl crossing three paces of air and stopping on iron as if she had been set there with a hand. There had been nothing on her shins.

Rhea had run a plate three times, a month ago, to be sure what a jam looked like. That had not been one.

*Not to be expected twice,* she had written. She had been right. It had not happened twice. This was a different thing, and it had happened once.

"Whose sheet's nearest?" she said.

"Old Four's." Hollis was at her elbow with his slate. "Their catcher's a third-year. He's waited two springs for this."

"Then he can have it."

The quiet boy was kneeling at his hem with a face like paper. She walked round the back of the string, a long pace off, and stopped.

"Catcher?"

He started as if she had struck him. She saw him swallow. He looked at his canvas, and at the cinder, and not once at her.

"Not yet," he said. It shook. "It's two paces north of me. I need it two paces north."

"Mace."

"Step. Step."

"Now."

Her left foot came down. Her right.

She had never had a Stride. She had stood under knots for twenty-three years. She raised her fist and let the blade be as long as it needed to be, a line of white from her knuckles to the place behind the frost, and took it away again.

Sixty drams came down. The skin slid.

It did it again, as it had for the boy. It tipped and went down one rung and sat on the next knot, and it was thicker.

"Dark. One."

The line drew off. Ten paces down the floor, three wards and a student's shoulder stood burning in the dead grey morning, and the blind thing swayed, and walked to them, and leaned.

*It likes a gate.*

Somebody had told sixteen children to put out every light on this floor. It was inside the book. Hollis had let them, and written it down, and she knew him well enough to know that he would have his own hide for it before the board did. She would have left a wall. So would he.

Neither of them would have guessed what it would find instead. She was honest enough to grant that.

She cut the third. She cut the fourth, with a student team from the east wall bagging under Bettan's eye, and on the fourth she heard Imrie's breath go ragged.

"Take," said Imrie.

Rhea was looking at the knot. She did not see the join. She listened for it, as she always did, and for ten slow counts there was a place in the Graywater wall that was held by a seventeen-year-old in a laundry coat.

She did not hear it.

"Give," said the girl. Her voice was steady. "Yours."

"Mine."

*Well,* thought Rhea, and cut the fifth.

By the sixth she could not find the edge of the skin by eye.

It had come down five rungs without breaking, and every rung it had kept what it had. It lay on the reader now like a second knot, a shell as thick as her wrist. A blade went in behind the edge of a skin. This one had hardly any behind left.

She found it by ear. She stood under it with her fist dark and listened to it ring on Mace's wall, and where the note was truest, she put the line.

It went. But it had been lit for a full second. She had not been lit for a full second since the Ferren floods.

"Two left," said Hollis. "The sit-down. I've no second blade, Rhea. Mine's at Brickfields with a broken wrist, and I'll not put a student under that."

"No."

She looked at what remained. It was low and wide, six legs and two great knots in its belly, side by side. The skin had come down with nowhere further to go. It lay over both of them like a lid on a pan.

Two knots. The book said two blades, together, or it would fall wherever it liked.

She had cut two with one line twice in her life. Once it had not worked, and a catcher had lost a foot.

"I want it turned," she said. "Broadside to me. Both knots in a row, edge on, so that one line goes through the pair. Mace, walk your wall round to the east. Slowly. Bettan, I want a double sheet under it, and I want your word and nobody else's."

"You'll have it when I've got it," said Bettan.

It took the best part of a minute. The wall went round in an arc, a step at a time, and the low grey thing shuffled after it, turning. Rhea stood with her fist at her side and did nothing whatever in front of eleven hundred people. She had long since stopped minding that.

She had time to look south.

There were two of them on the shelf. The red-haired one was standing against the brick with her arms folded. The other sat with her back to the wall and one leg stuck out over the ditch, and there was blood on her chin.

Her fist was closed at her collar.

Rhea had been nineteen. She had been in brown water to her chest, in a culvert, in the dark, and a tall woman with a scarred jaw had got an arm round her and held on. With the other hand that woman had been gripping something at her own throat, hard, as a person grips a rail.

She had never asked what Ilse Corran felt in her fist that night. She did not know what the girl felt in hers.

She knew what it looked like from across a floor.

"Now," said Bettan.

Her left foot. Her right.

The line went out level, at the height of her hip, as long as a tram pole. It went in at the edge of the shell and through one knot and through the other, and she held it lit until she felt both open. A second. A second and a half.

It sat down.

It went onto the double sheet with a sigh, all of it, and four catchers were on it. One of them was a student, with brass on his collar and tears running openly down his face, hauling a cord.

Rhea let the metal run home. Her arm ached to the shoulder.

She took a card from her coat while the count was still open, and a stub of pencil, and wrote on it standing up.

*Flask, the Pit, the 26th. Company left no gate; string took the bus. Rowan opened the south link on two doors, one after the other. Nothing dropped between. About ten seconds. Then down. That is not what I saw on the plate. Still NOT for draft. Attach Seven to Graywater. I'll take the shift myself. — R.S.*

She read it over. She did not turn it and write on the back. There was a private card in her other pocket that had been there for six weeks, with two words on it.

She thought that she might not be able to keep it private very much longer.

---

The keeper came through his iron door four minutes and nine seconds after he had left his stool.

He stood in the doorway with his keys in his fist, heaving like a bellows, and looked at two students on his shelf and a lever pinned level in its lower eye.

"Who brought that down?"

"She did," said Selka Dray. "I hung on it."

Dunmore looked at Kiva's strapped knee. He looked at the three paces of sump. He opened his mouth, and shut it, and took a brass padlock off his ring and put it through the pin.

"I'll want that in my book," he said. "I don't know what I'll write."

He took them out by the road stair. It was sixty-one steps. Dray went up them behind Kiva with one hand an inch from her belt, not touching it, and said nothing the whole way.

At the top, in the daylight, with the Brickfields road in front of them and the Pit roaring behind the wall, she spoke.

"I've not changed my mind."

"I didn't think you had."

"You were on a floor and a branch went dark. Again. I'll be writing that down." The red head was up. Her face was pale and set. "You called that floor dark. All of it. I was at the rail. I heard whose notion it was."

"It was mine."

"Yes." Dray seemed thrown by having it handed to her. She recovered. "I'd have pinned that lever for anybody. I want that understood. It's nothing to do with you."

"I know. Thank you for counting."

"Somebody had to," said Selka Dray, and walked away down the road toward the keeper's gate.

Kiva put her hand inside her collar.

The disk was cooling. She could feel it go. By the time she had limped the length of the wall to the gate, it was the cold of a coin on a windowsill, and she could not have proved to anyone that it had ever been otherwise.

---

They had bagged the last of it before she reached the table.

She did not see it done. She saw the floor afterward: a dark arena under a bright sky, with seven canvas bags in a row along the east wall and the Kiln shut behind its iron. Graywater's people were carrying their coats. A healer's cart stood at the west gate.

Her own bag lay on the trestle where Perrin had set it down.

"It's to be panned inside the hour," said the clerk in the apron. He had not left his post. He was a brave boy, she thought, or a very frightened one. "It's the rule. I've been keeping the time. You've eleven minutes."

Kiva held up her left hand. It was shaking like a leaf on a twig, and the first two fingers were the color of candle wax.

"I can't."

"Then tell us how," said Anwen.

She had come down the floor with her coat open and her hair stuck to her forehead. Kellan was behind her. Behind him, walking very carefully, with Lowen Brask's hand under his elbow, came Perrin.

"I said don't pan it without me," said Perrin. "I've been thinking about it for a quarter of an hour. It's all that's kept me upright."

Kiva sat on an upturned crate and called it.

It was the strangest catch of her life. She did not touch a grain. Anwen had the right pan and Kellan the left, and he held it as he had held a corner of canvas, with deep suspicion. Perrin cut cords with his teeth. She told them when to fill and when to level, and she listened for the lids.

"Seal check."

"Sound," said Anwen.

"That one isn't. I heard it. Kellan, turn it over."

He turned it over. A thread of grey ran out along the rim.

"How did you hear that?" he said.

"It didn't click. Do it again."

It made nine tins, and part of a tenth. Bettan came with her brass balance and set them on the pan one at a time, moving the little weights with a fingernail.

"Fifty-one," she said. "And two-tenths."

The mark was fifty.

Nobody cheered. Perrin sat down on the cinder. Kellan looked at the nine and a bit tins as though they had been a board he could not make add up.

"We called it dark," he said. "The whole floor. It walked off and found the bus, and a woman's hands are burned, and a branch is out. And we've passed."

"Your rung's passed," said Hollis Vane.

He had come up behind Bettan. He looked ten years older than he had at ten o'clock, and he held the slate out where the four of them could read it.

*T7. Rung one: hold, cut, bag sealed at 47. 51.2 on the table, to grade. Nothing printed. Co. left no gate lit; string took the race; event struck 10:22. Field direction 10:23: Rowan to S. link, two doors in sequence, declared; link opened; held till pinned (Dray). Hurt: Rowan, collapse after. Cade, ribs, again. Rung passed, subject to the bag. The rest is for the board.*

"I'll say this once," he said. "I told you that I'd leave a gate, and I let you not. It was inside the book. I didn't know a string could take a bus. Nobody did. That's my line on the slate as much as yours, and I'll be saying so in a room on Friday." His pale eyes went to Kiva. "You told me it was keeping time. I looked at that board twenty seconds sooner than I would have. I've been trying to work out what I'd have done with twenty seconds less."

She had no voice to answer him with.

"Kiva." Anwen was kneeling in front of the crate. Her palms were an inch above Kiva's chest, and her face had gone still and inward. "The lines aren't burned. They're—I don't know what they are. Tired. But your hand. When did those fingers go white?"

"On the lever."

"That's forty minutes." Anwen opened her eyes. "Aske said the same hour. Not the next day. I was in the room." She turned her head. "Kellan. Where's the team book?"

"In my coat."

"We've a dram, sealed, with her name on what it's for. I want it signed out. All four. Now." She was already getting up. "I want that said."

"It's said," said Perrin, from the ground. "Somebody help me find a pencil."

Kiva looked across the dark floor.

A woman was coming over it from the keeper's gate with a canvas-backed book under her arm, walking fast. Beside her, on a stick, slower, came another.

She did not know which of them she wanted to tell first. She found that she was going to have to tell them both.

---

## CHAPTER — books/book-2680-opening/manuscript/chapter-24.md

# Chapter 24 — Kiva: What Was Tinned

Healer Aske had the coldest thumbs in Meridian, and on Wednesday morning they were in the back of a cart.

It was the Wren House cart that stood at the west gate on every Kiln day, a covered wagon with two cots and a locked chest. On one cot lay Greta Stroud, grey in the face, with both her hands wrapped to the wrists in wet cloth. Kiva was put on the other.

"Left hand," said Aske. "Palm up. Don't help me."

The cold thumbs went along her first two fingers, joint by joint. Kiva watched them press. She felt the third finger and the fourth. She felt the first two some while afterward, faintly, as one hears a neighbor through a wall.

"When?"

"On the lever. Forty minutes ago. A little more."

"Then you're inside the hour, and somebody remembered what was said." The grey eyes went briefly to Anwen, who was standing at the tailboard with the team book open in both hands. "Is that page signed?"

"Four names. One dram, ward-trace, sealed at the ash office. To Healer Aske, for K. Rowan." Anwen held it out. "She signed with her right."

"That's what the page is for. I ease now, and I draw against it tomorrow." Aske unlocked the chest. "Sleeve."

The ash came out of a jar no bigger than an egg cup. It was the pale silver-grey that Kiva had seen once before, in a grading cup, and Aske did not settle it. The healer laid it along the inside of Kiva's forearm in a thin line from the elbow to the wrist, and then set both hands over it, and shut those grey eyes.

It was like having a cold, wet string drawn slowly through the arm.

It did not hurt. It went down from the elbow, through the wrist, and out along the two dull fingers to their tips. Where it passed, the deep ache went quiet. The trembling in her hand slowed, and stopped.

"There," said Aske, after a long time. The ash on her arm had gone the color of chalk. "Now. I told you a month ago that this nerve had been burned once and wouldn't forgive a second. I've been looking for the second. It isn't there."

Kiva let her breath go.

"It isn't burned. It's starved. Something went through that arm this morning a great deal faster than it has ever been asked to carry anything, and it took what there was. I don't know what you did. I'm told half the Compact watched and nobody else knows either." The lid of the jar went on. "They'll sulk for a week. Then they'll be where they were on Tuesday, which is slow, and late, and not finished mending. No better. I want that understood. I've eased it. I haven't cured anything."

"And if I'd come tomorrow?"

"Then I'd be saying something else, and you'd not have liked it." Aske turned to the other cot. "A fortnight. No lanes, no floors, nothing lit. The knee was already owed that. Tell your captain."

Greta Stroud had turned her head on the pillow. Her broken nose was very white.

"You're the one on the shelf," she said hoarsely.

"Yes."

"I'd my eyes shut for most of it. They tell me it turned round and walked off." She looked down at her wrapped hands. "I slid a wall under a Flask because a woman I'd follow anywhere said it might do. It was a fair notion. I'd have had it myself." Her mouth twisted. "Your girl stood my place in the line for ten. Mace says he never heard the join."

"She's not my girl," said Kiva. "She's her own."

"Good," said Stroud, and shut her eyes.

---

They were waiting at the foot of the cart.

Mara had the canvas-backed book under her arm. Orla had both hands on the stick. Neither had tried to climb the step. Kiva got down it by herself, slowly, and stood between them in the thin sun with her sleeve still rolled.

She told them.

She had meant to choose her words. There were not enough of them to choose among. She said that the disk had been warm. She said when, and for how long, and what her fist had been doing. She said that it was cold again by the keeper's gate.

Then she told them about the shelf going away.

Mara's face did not change. It had not changed in a hallway at a quarter to three in the morning either. Only her hand moved, and came to rest flat on the cover of the book, as it rested on a case she was reading.

"Standing up," she said. "In daylight. Over an open sump."

"Dray had me by the belt."

"Then I owe that girl a thing I don't know how to pay." She breathed. "The same as before?"

"There was sound. I'll tell you all of it tonight. At the table. That's the rule."

"It is." Mara looked at her for a moment longer. "I was in the pump house. I had my hand on cell nine. The bank went into step at twenty-two minutes past ten, all four-and-twenty, like a room full of clocks. It came out of it at twenty-four." She tapped the book. "Not one fouled. The pumps never dropped a stroke. I wrote that down before I knew who'd done it."

Orla had said nothing at all.

She was looking at the place where the cord crossed Kiva's collarbone. Her crooked face was perfectly still.

"Is it warm now?"

"No."

"Did you ask it for anything?"

"No. I'd nothing to ask."

"Did it tell you anything?"

"No." Kiva thought. "It was only warm. Like a hand."

Orla nodded, very slowly. She did not put out her own hand to feel. Kiva noticed that.

"Then you'll write it tonight, before you sleep," she said. "The hour. How long. What you'd just finished doing, in what order. Not one word of what you think it means. I'll want to read that page, and I'll want it to be only what happened."

"You know something."

"I know there's something to know. That isn't the same, and I've been wrong before by treating it as though it were." Orla shifted her weight on the stick. "I saw your shins from the rail. Bare. I saw your foot come down. I saw that lever start."

"Will you sign it?"

"No."

It was so prompt that Kiva nearly laughed. It set her nose off again.

"I've seen it once," said Orla. "Once is an accident with good manners. You told a Home Line officer you'd missed fourteen, in front of every assessor in the district, and then you made the fifteenth. I'm prouder of the first half than I can easily say. But my name goes on things I can ask for twice." She turned toward the gate. "Show me six."

---

The fever came after dark.

It was not like the last. That had been hot sand in the lines. This was a slow heat everywhere, a banked stove, and a headache that sat behind her eyes. She lay on top of the blanket with a wet cloth across her chest and the disk cold on the cloth.

There was no dream. She waited for one, and it did not come.

There was only the note.

She could not hum it. She tried, quietly, with her teeth shut, and what came out was not it. But it was there, the way the after-ring of a bell is there. It was one note that had been a great many.

She got up at ten and went down the passage to the kitchen. The rule said the same night.

Mara had the lamp trimmed and a clean page.

Kiva told it. She told the height, and the roads, and the lights that were all unlike. She told the notes. She told about trying to count, and losing four. She told the lights touching, and the one note, and looking up.

"And under it," she said. "At the very end. Something small. It ticked. Once."

Mara's pencil stopped.

"Like the bag."

"Like the bag. Like a drum pen." Kiva pressed the cloth to her chest. "I don't know that it's the same. I only know it's the word I'd use for both."

"Then that's what I'll write. *The word she'd use for both.*" Mara wrote it. She sat back. "I looked at the hall panel when we came in. Did you see me?"

"Yes."

"It's quenched. It's been quenched since the thirteenth. There's nothing in it to touch. I looked anyway." She was quiet. "I shall probably go on looking."

The stove ticked. Kiva drank the water that had been set out for her.

"Orla told me what you said to him," said Mara. "The officer. Before you went."

"That I'd missed."

"Fourteen times. Out loud. With the Chair in the front row." Mara turned the pencil end over end on the table. "I signed a paper on an embankment six weeks ago. I've wondered every night since whether I'd signed it because I believed you or because I couldn't bear to be the one who said no." She set the pencil down. "I heard a girl tell eleven hundred people exactly what she couldn't do, and then be let do it by somebody whose business it was to decide. That's a meter, and a shutdown path, and a book. I couldn't have asked for more if I'd drawn it up myself."

"I was frightened."

"I should hope so. So was I. I was across the road with my hand on a cell." She pushed the water jug nearer. "Drink that. And then you'll help me with something, if your head will stand it. I've the keeper's book for Wednesday. I'm to reconcile it."

It lay under the brass weight, a smaller book than the trunk's, black with handling.

"Not tonight," said Mara. "Saturday. I want to read it twice by myself first. There's a line in it I'd like to be sure of before I show it to anyone."

---

Grader Halloran cut the first cord on Thursday morning and looked at what was in the tin for rather a long time.

The Wren House grading room was as it had been a month ago, with no lamp or cell within thirty paces, and seven cups, and the long shelf of jars. Team Seven stood in a row before the bench. Perrin had been strapped again from the armpits to the hips, and stood like a pillar.

"Fifty-one and two-tenths," said Halloran. "Agreed with the table. Now the cup."

A pinch went into the test cell. The grader laid a slow, plain tune on it and began to count.

Kiva counted too. Sixty. That was Second. Their Handful had held eighty-one, with a cinder in it. Eighty. Ninety.

At a hundred, Kellan's hand closed on the edge of the bench.

"A hundred and eighteen," said Halloran, and lifted the hand away. "That's First. By a clear margin. I've not graded a student rung First since the year my daughter was born." A dry look went along the row. "What took the last one off First, Mr. Renn? You asked me that in this room."

"Cinder. From the path."

"And this was caught on waxed canvas and bagged in forty-seven. So it would seem that somebody learned." The grader took up the comb.

It was the brass frame with nine reeds. Halloran held it over the open tin, as before, and Kiva watched the third reed from the left begin to shiver.

It did not hum faintly, as it had for a trace. It sang. It was a clear thin steady note that filled the bench.

"Ward," said Halloran. "Full. Not a trace. The whole rung's bent that way. I've an infirmary upstairs that would take this tin out of my hands before I'd finished writing the card. There's nothing kinder to a nerve or a burn." The grader's eyes went to Kiva's left sleeve and away. "As I gather somebody's found."

Then Halloran frowned.

The comb had not been taken away. Under the third reed's song, there was another sound. It was low and dry and very small. Kiva looked along the brass.

The seventh reed was moving.

It was one of the four that had no name. It did not sing. It tapped. It touched its neighbor and came away, and touched, and came away.

"Now that," said Halloran softly, "I've heard twice in nineteen years."

Nobody breathed.

"Both times it was ash swept out of a cell that had been salted late. A tune leaves that. Something that's been made to keep step." The comb turned a little in those careful fingers. "The shelf's cards call it *I.* For induction. I'd never thought to hold it over anything that came out of a rift." A pause. "This was cut and bagged before the string went near your race?"

"Forty-seven counts after the cut," said Kiva. "It was still on our sheet. It hadn't moved."

"Then it brought that with it."

"I felt it. Inside the count. I told the foreman it was keeping time."

"Did you." Halloran wrote on a fresh card, in a small upright hand, and read it aloud while writing. "*Flask, rung one, skinned. Yield 51.2. Grade First. Resonance: W, full. I, trace. Catcher reports 'keeping time' inside the count.*" The card was propped against the tin. "I'm not going to tell you what that means. I grade ash. I don't grade springs. But there's been a man in black and gold in my passage since eight o'clock, asking very civilly what weight I'd put on a seventh reed. I thought you ought to hear it from me before you hear it from him."

---

Ysra Crest read the board's findings on Friday as she read everything, from the first word to the last.

They were all in her office. There were not chairs enough. Team Seven stood, and Orla sat by the window, and Hollis Vane leaned on the wall beside the door with his slate turned inward against his coat.

"*Was it closed,*" said Ysra. "The Kiln is on its lid. The branch was restored at four that afternoon under a Home Line wall, and the lattice took back what was behind it. The Pit is shut for a fortnight." She turned the page. "*Who was hurt.* A chartered ward-holder, both hands, six weeks. Two students. *What still works.* The Ferren pumps. That is underlined, and not by me. *What was tinned.* Four hundred and nineteen drams, of which fifty-one and a fifth were bagged by this team before anything went wrong."

She laid the page down.

"The board finds as follows. The fifth company left no gate lit. That was inside the Circuit's book and outside the Home Line's custom. The foreman said he would leave one, and recorded that he had said it. Neither the book nor the custom foresaw a string that could take a bus." Her level eyes went to the door. "Officer Vane asked that his own name stand first in that paragraph. It does."

Vane said nothing. He inclined his head.

"The book has been changed. As of last night there is a rule. *A gate shall be left lit.* It has Miss Rowan's floor to thank for its existence, and I should not, in her place, take that as a compliment."

"I don't," said Kiva.

"No. I see that you don't." Ysra took up the next sheet. "The second step. Your rung passed its mark, clean, at First. Your company failed its third column until three professionals and a keeper retrieved it. The board will not grant a full step on that, and it will not withhold one from a team that bagged fifty-one. So it has invented something, which is the board's habit." She read. "*Second step, attached.* Each team of the fifth company may answer a Flask-yield call only as an attached recovery and support team, under a chartered crew, until it has stood one whole Flask under a foreman without remark."

"Attached to whom?" said Kellan.

"Old Four goes to the Home Line. Officer Vane asked for them." Ysra turned the sheet over. "You were asked for as well. By name, by the senior Edge of the Graywater Company, on a draft card, with the words *I'll take the shift myself.* I have rarely seen that officer write a sentence she did not have to."

Anwen made a small sound. Kiva did not look at her.

*She looked at you twice,* Perrin had said, on a river bank.

"Now the rest, and it is less agreeable. Eleven teams were to have qualified on Wednesday afternoon and Thursday. They will wait for a held tear. Several have written to me, and they have not written kindly. Miss Dray has renewed her objection. She has it on evidence this time, as I told her she must." Ysra folded her hands. "She also pinned a link, and says in her second line that the one has no bearing on the other. I am inclined to agree with her on that point. I have not ruled on the rest. I shall hear it when I have the Pit's tape in front of me."

She looked at Kiva last.

"Your conditions. Officer Vane struck the event at twenty-two minutes past ten. He gave you a field direction a minute later, with a reason, and your sponsor took the consequence upon herself in the hearing of the whole ground. Nothing that followed occurred in a sanctioned event. There is therefore no incident. I looked for one." A pause. "Your place is as it was. Your certificate is as it was. Your sponsor has added nothing to it, and has told me why, and I thought the reason sound."

"Six," said Orla, from the window.

"So I understand." Ysra put the papers into the folder. "You are still provisional Third Division, Miss Rowan. You are certified to kneel. On Wednesday you did something that nobody in this building can classify, and it cost more ash for its length than any act on that mast's drum. Mr. Bexley has shown me. I should like you to leave this room hating both of those facts equally."

"I'll try."

"I have every confidence," said Ysra Crest.

---

The ash office had a new line on its posted list. *Purse sheet and poles: 3.0.* Kiva saw it on the way in, and did not think that anybody had put it there for them.

The clerk did the sum on a slate. It came out as evenly as the first had.

*Yield, 51.2. Civic, half: 25.6. Casualty reserve, a quarter of the remainder: 6.4. Schools, half of what is left: 9.6. Team: 9.6.*

Below it was the line that the board had drawn across the standings.

*T7. Total yield, 72.0. Ninth of thirty-one. (att.)*

"Ninth," said Perrin. "With brackets."

"I like the brackets," said Kellan. "They're accurate."

They took the team book home to the Rowan kitchen on Saturday evening, because that was where the first share had been divided, and because Perrin could no longer get down the laundry's back steps without language.

Mara was at the table when Kiva came in ahead of the others. She had the keeper's black book open, and two sheets of copying paper beside it.

"Sit," she said. "I've read it three times. I'm sure of it."

She turned the book round.

It was the Pit's branch book for Wednesday the twenty-sixth, in a shaky, old-fashioned hand. *Kiln opened 10:12. All circuits in step, 10:22. Link opened from the floor, 10:24. All dark.* And then a line by itself.

*10:24, later by about forty seconds. Lamp ring. Every lamp, once. Branch isolated. Link pinned. No current. No fault found. T-0. A.D.*

Kiva read it upside down, and then the right way.

"Dray said they jumped."

"Dunmore saw it from the road stair. He wrote *T-0* because he's kept that book for twenty years and didn't know what else a man writes." Mara's finger rested beside the line. "I've copied three of those for you, out of registers older than this street. I never thought I'd be handed one with the ink hardly dry." She drew one of the loose sheets across. "And this came from Hanley Street on Friday. The clerk's finished."

It was the entry for 2446. Kiva had waited six weeks for it.

It was three lines. *Night. Every lamp, every district. No fault found.* She had known that much. But the clerk had copied the margin as well, faithfully, because he was that sort of clerk. In the margin there was a note in a different hand, older and cramped. He had not been able to read it. He had drawn it.

It was a row of marks, like a line of bent pins.

"He says it's a record hand that went out two hundred years ago," said Mara. "He says there's a woman at the Compact Library who can read it, in the superseded stacks, if anyone asks her nicely." She squared the two sheets, one on the other. "I don't know what I've got here, Kiva. I know that I'd not put either of these down as nothing."

There were feet on the stair. Kiva put both papers inside the field book, and shut it.

---

"Nine and six-tenths," said Kellan. "Graded First. I've a list."

"So have I," said Anwen. She set hers beside his. For once she looked at it. "Mine's short. Two drams sealed. Before anything. The same as last time, only twice, because we've seen what one's worth." Her eyes went to Kiva's left hand, lying on the table. "It stays ward. Nobody breathes on it."

Nobody argued. Perrin wrote it down.

"One back to the Wren House," said Kiva. "To the infirmary. At par." She had thought about it in the cart. "There's a woman upstairs with both hands burned. Her company will pay for her easing; I know that. I'd still like it to be ours. We called the floor dark."

"*You* called it," said Perrin. "We put our hands up. I've been wanting to say that since Wednesday. I'd like it written that I voted."

"It's written. I've your hand in the book."

"Good. One to the infirmary. That's three."

"A sheet," said Kellan. "Our own, with poles. Three. I'm not bagging my next rung in a canvas I've had to sign for at a gate."

"Six."

Then there was a silence. They had all known that it was coming.

"Two," said Kellan. He said it to the list. "For the yard. For her."

Kiva had her mouth open already.

"It's a third of a dram a go. For nine seconds. Noa's shown me the tape. It's the worst Draw anybody's ever put on a sanctioned mast, and it went to open a lever. I can't ask you—"

"You haven't asked. I've put it on my list." He looked up. His face was as it had been in a cold room, saying *me.* "It's also the only thing anybody at this table can do that nobody else in the Compact can. I stood on that floor with a Flask in front of me and nothing to cut, and I carried a woman to a gate. I was glad to. It was what there was." He frowned. "You were the one thing on that ground that fitted. I'd like to know whether it was luck. Two drams is six goes, at that price. I'm told your sponsor wants six."

"She does."

"Then it comes out exact."

"I'd like it said," said Perrin slowly, "that I mind."

They looked at him.

"I do. I held a capstan on two ribs that had nearly mended. I'm strapped like a parcel. Nobody's voting me a dram to practice walking in a circle." He drew a careful breath. "And I vote yes. I've decided I'm allowed both; she taught me. It's two drams to find out what one of us is. I'd pay more than that to know what I am."

"Anwen?" said Kiva.

Anwen had been turning her pencil.

"I'm going," she said. "I told Mace this morning. Six weeks from the tenth. I wanted to say it here first and I couldn't find the place." She set the pencil down. "So I'm voting to spend ash on a yard I shan't be standing in, and a sheet I shan't hold a corner of till the autumn. I want that said. And I want it said that I'd vote the same if I were staying."

"It's said."

"Then yes. On three conditions, and you'll write them, Kiva, in your own hand. A sanctioned mast, every go. Orla on the crate. And Noa reads it after and never during." She almost smiled. "You taught me to write conditions."

"That's eight," said Perrin. "One and six-tenths over."

"Held," said Kiva. "On the book. Four-tenths a head, for the next time one of us is dry in a lane." She had run dry at the Terrace, on two hundredths, because she had asked for nothing. "I was wrong about that in the spring."

Kellan wrote the last line. He turned the book.

*Sealed, medical, W: 2.0. Returned, Wren House infirmary, at par: 1.0. Purse sheet and poles: 3.0. Sanctioned practice, two-door, K. Rowan, on conditions: 2.0. Held, 0.4 a head: 1.6. Total 9.6.*

They signed. It took four names, as it always did.

"The Halden man found me," said Kellan, when the pencil had come back to him. He did not look up. "On Thursday, outside the grading room. A license team of theirs, for the Regional. A stipend. He named the figure."

"What did you say?"

"That I'd compare." He folded his paper. "I've not finished. I thought you should all know that I'm doing the sum."

Perrin laughed, and held his side.

"Brask's been offered a summer on a Home Line hold crew," he said. "Vane asked him at the capstan, with the lid still warm. And the Hall's master has my name down for the second team in the autumn, on Brask's word. *The only one who didn't have to be told twice.* I'm to have another go at the plate when I can breathe." He shook his head. "A month ago, we'd one share and nowhere to go. Now there's four of us and I can't find two going the same way."

Nobody said anything to that. Outside, the lamp at the corner of Coper Street came on.

---

Noa Bexley had the tape pinned along the edge of a drawing board, and he had been looking at it for two days.

He was in the mast room at the Pit, a brick cupboard under the eastern rim. The keeper had let him in with a grunt. The Chair's letter said *sanctioned instrument,* and *copy to this desk,* and Noa had made the copy on Thursday, and then he had gone on sitting there.

Sixteen pens. He had looked at fifteen of them once.

The sixteenth lay dead flat along its line from the gate until nearly twenty-four minutes past ten. He had pointed at it on Wednesday morning. *Yours. Doing nothing.* A whole dram, unspent, for ninety minutes.

Then it moved.

There was a small neat step. That was the bound. He had measured a great many bounds, his own among them, and that one was two hundredths, as it ought to be.

After the step the pen should have run level. She had shut the Ember at the top. Everyone said so. She said so. Shut meant nothing drawing.

It did not run level.

It went straight up the paper.

He had put a glass on it. The line climbed twenty-six hundredths in a fifth of a second. There was no slope to it. It was a cliff. The pen had gone up so hard that it had left a tiny blot at the top, where the nib had jarred.

And then, from the top of the cliff, a gentle slope. Nine seconds and a little, three hundredths, a tired stance on a heavy lever. That was all.

"Two, and twenty-six, and three," said Noa aloud. "Thirty-one."

He had written it into her book on Thursday, in the cart shed, and into his own. He had worked the Draw because he could not stop himself, and it had come out near twelve. The bench had frightened a Placement Chair with three point one.

Twelve. For the only thing she had ever done that worked.

It was the cliff that he could not leave alone. The mast drew a pen for a fighter, not a door. It could not say what had been open. But he had the keeper's word, and the foreman's slate, and a Graywater Edge's draft card that the Chair had let him read, and they all said the same. Her shins had been bare. Her foot had not yet come down.

By every account, there had been nothing open at all when twenty-six hundredths went out of her.

*The work is in me in the air,* she had written. He had seen it. She let him read the *why* column now. *Where is it when it isn't in either one?*

He did not know. He knew what it cost to get it across.

He turned to the notebook's other page. *Things that are probably nothing.* It had grown. He read down it, as he did most nights. The tears. The skins. The class. Her still door. A plate in a meadow. A seventh reed.

He wrote at the bottom, in pencil.

*The Pit, 26th. Every lamp on a dead ring, once. Keeper's book says T-0. She has three more of those in hers.*

"Mr. Bexley?"

He had not heard the door.

The man was about forty, and pleasant, and wore a black coat with a narrow gold cord at the cuff. He stood with his hat in his hand as if the mast room had been somebody's parlor.

"I'm told you're the one who reads for the Chair. I'm with the Halden technical office. We've an interest in efficient patterning, as you'd expect." He smiled. "I understand that there's a tape."

"There's a sanctioned tape," said Noa. His mouth was dry. "It goes to the Placement Chair. I can't show it."

"Of course not. And your own notes? I'm told you've a paper in hand. We fund a good many papers. It can make a difference to a young man in Third Division." The smile did not alter. "Your name would be first on it, naturally."

Noa looked at the drawing board. He looked at the cliff, with its tiny blot.

He had wanted a paper since he was eleven. He had wanted it in the roster corridor, among the drawers, so badly that he had had to sit down. *Second,* he had told her, at the foot of a stone porch. *After yours.*

"They're her numbers," he said.

"I beg your pardon?"

"Every one of them. I measure. I don't own." It came out steadier than he had expected. "She sees the sheet first, and her sponsor does. If she says stop, I stop. You'd have to ask her. I don't think you'd like what her aunt would say."

"I see." The pleasant man put his hat on. "The Circuit's tapes are open to chartered parties thirty days after the standings post. You'll know that. So I shall be reading this one on the twenty-eighth of next month, in any case." He nodded at the board. "I only thought it might be agreeable to read it in company. Good evening."

The door shut.

Noa sat for a while with his hands flat on his knees.

Thirty days. Anybody with a charter could walk in here in thirty days and put a glass on that cliff. They would not need him. They would not need her leave.

He took the pencil, and under the last line on the page that was probably nothing, he wrote one more.

*They can look now. So I'd better find out first.*

---

The Old Yard was the same on Sunday night as it had always been.

Kiva had come on the tram with the book under her arm and nothing settled in her. She was not to light so much as a lamp for a fortnight. Orla had sent word anyway. *Bring the page.*

She gave it over, open at Wednesday.

Orla read it by the lamp on its hook. She read it twice. Kiva sat on the other crate with her leg out and watched the crooked face, and could tell nothing.

*Disk: warm. Began after I shut the still door, with the lever on its pin. Not before. Lasted from the shelf to the top of the road stair. Cold by the keeper's gate.*

*Did not ask it anything. It did not do anything.*

*Then the other thing, about three counts. See Mara's page. Dray says the lamps jumped. Keeper's book says T-0, 10:24 and forty seconds.*

"You've kept to it," said Orla at last. "There's not a guess on here."

"You told me not to."

"I did. I wasn't sure you could." She closed the book and held it on her knee. "I'll tell you what I've got, then, since you've been honest. It isn't much. There's a book at the Home Line hall. A bearer's book. I've known for thirty years that it existed, and I have never once been on the list of people who may ask to see it."

Kiva sat very still.

"Ilse Corran was on that list. She wrote in it, I think. I don't know what." The stick turned slowly in Orla's hands. "Hollis Vane is on it. He's watch officer; they all are. I took that man down his first culvert and fell in it, and he's owed me a favor for twenty-five years. I'd been saving it. I asked him for it on Thursday."

"What did he say?"

"He said that he'd been about to come and ask me." Orla nearly smiled. "It seems that an officer who gives a field direction to an unclassified candidate has to write a reason in a ledger older than this school. He went to write his. He found that somebody had been there before him."

"When?"

"I don't know. He wouldn't tell me in a corridor." She handed the book back. "I'm not promising you an answer, Rowan. I've read things in old books before and been no wiser. I'm telling you that there's a page somewhere, and that for the first time I've a reason good enough to ask for it."

Kiva opened the field book at the back. She took out the two sheets and held them to the light.

A keeper's line, four days old. A clerk's copy of a line two hundred and thirty-four years old, with a margin full of bent pins.

"There's a woman at the Compact Library who can read this hand," she said. "In the superseded stacks. Mara says so."

Orla looked at the marks for a long moment.

"I know those stacks," she said. Her voice was odd. "I've been in them. I read a thing there once that I've told nobody, because I'd no right to think it had to do with you." She gave the sheet back, with care. "I think I may have to stop being so sure of that. Not tonight. Tonight you'll fill in your columns."

The lamp hissed. Down the lane, the last post leaned out of its row into the dark.

Kiva opened the book on her good knee.

*Exercise:* Flask qualifier, the Pit. Fifth company. Rung one.

*What went wrong:* I called the whole floor dark. The foreman said he'd leave a gate and I didn't. I set the mark for the loudest thing and never asked what was nearest. Walked off with my own bag and didn't watch the string.

*Who was hurt:* Greta Stroud, both hands. Perrin, ribs again. Me.

*What it cost:* A branch, a fortnight. Eleven teams their day. 0.31 of a dram in nine seconds. The sealed dram, for my hand.

*How long to come back:* Fingers, a week, to where they were. Knee, the second. The rest I don't know.

*Second go:* Told him what I couldn't do. Was let. Shut the first at the top. Set the second like a pan. Counted through it. Gave it to Dray when she said *mine.*

She stopped at the last column. She thought about a brick lane, and a line of chalk on a slate, and a reason that had won on Saturday and fouled a grid on Wednesday.

*Why:* A man with nothing in front of him waits. A husk goes looking. I'd been fighting a man.

That was true. It was not all of it.

*The work goes across. It costs more than both doors. I don't know what carries it.*

She looked at that. Then, smaller, at the very foot of the page, where she had once written *kneeling:*

*Six. And find out who wrote it down.*

She shut the book, and sat with it on her knee in the yellow light.

The disk was cold. She did not take it out to see. She knew what it weighed, and what it had felt like for the length of a road stair, and that there had been people before her who had known the same and put it on a page.

She did not think that any of them had been told why, either.

But somebody had kept the page.

---
